  * [ไทย](https://th.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%87%E0%B8%A7%E0%B8%B1%E0%B8%A5%E0%B8%AD%E0%B8%AD%E0%B8%AA%E0%B8%81%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%A3%E0%B9%8C_%E0%B8%AA%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%82%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%A0%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%9E%E0%B8%A2%E0%B8%99%E0%B8%95%E0%B8%A3%E0%B9%8C%E0%B8%A2%E0%B8%AD%E0%B8%94%E0%B9%80%E0%B8%A2%E0%B8%B5%E0%B9%88%E0%B8%A2%E0%B8%A1 "รางวัลออสการ์ สาขาภาพยนตร์ยอดเยี่ยม – Thai")
  * [Türkçe](https://tr.wikipedia.org/wiki/En_%C4%B0yi_Film_Akademi_%C3%96d%C3%BCl%C3%BC "En İyi Film Akademi Ödülü – Turkish")
  * [Татарча / tatarça](https://tt.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D0%98%D2%A3_%D1%8F%D1%85%D1%88%D1%8B_%D1%84%D0%B8%D0%BB%D1%8C%D0%BC_%D3%A9%D1%87%D0%B5%D0%BD_%C2%AB%D0%9E%D1%81%D0%BA%D0%B0%D1%80%C2%BB_%D0%BF%D1%80%D0%B5%D0%BC%D0%B8%D1%8F%D1%81%D0%B5 "Иң яхшы фильм өчен «Оскар» премиясе – Tatar")
  * [Українська](https://uk.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D0%9F%D1%80%D0%B5%D0%BC%D1%96%D1%8F_%C2%AB%D0%9E%D1%81%D0%BA%D0%B0%D1%80%C2%BB_%D0%B7%D0%B0_%D0%BD%D0%B0%D0%B9%D0%BA%D1%80%D0%B0%D1%89%D0%B8%D0%B9_%D1%84%D1%96%D0%BB%D1%8C%D0%BC "Премія «Оскар» за найкращий фільм – Ukrainian")
  * [اردو](https://ur.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D8%A7%DA%A9%DB%8C%DA%88%D9%85%DB%8C_%D8%A7%DB%8C%D9%88%D8%A7%D8%B1%DA%88_%D8%A8%D8%B1%D8%A7%D8%A6%DB%92_%D8%A8%DB%81%D8%AA%D8%B1%DB%8C%D9%86_%D9%81%D9%84%D9%85 "اکیڈمی ایوارڈ برائے بہترین فلم – Urdu")
  * [Oʻzbekcha / ўзбекча](https://uz.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eng_yaxshi_film_uchun_Oscar_mukofoti "Eng yaxshi film uchun Oscar mukofoti – Uzbek")

  * [中文](https://zh.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E5%A5%A5%E6%96%AF%E5%8D%A1%E6%9C%80%E4%BD%B3%E5%BD%B1%E7%89%87%E5%A5%96 "奥斯卡最佳影片奖 – Chinese")

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Annual award by the Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Sciences

Academy Award for Best Picture

The **Academy Award for Best Picture** is one of the [Academy Awards](/wiki/Academy_Awards "Academy Awards") (also known as Oscars) presented annually by the [Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Sciences](/wiki/Academy_of_Motion_Picture_Arts_and_Sciences "Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Sciences") (AMPAS) since the awards debuted on May 16, 1929. This award goes to the [producers](/wiki/Film_producer "Film producer") of the film and is the only category in which every member of the Academy is eligible to submit a nomination and vote on the final ballot.[1] The Best Picture category is traditionally the final award of the night and is widely considered the most prestigious honor of the ceremony.[2][3][4]

The Grand Staircase columns at the [Dolby Theatre](/wiki/Dolby_Theatre "Dolby Theatre") in [Hollywood](/wiki/Hollywood,_Los_Angeles,_California "Hollywood, Los Angeles, California"), where the Academy Awards ceremonies have been held since 2002, showcase every film that has won the Best Picture title since the award's inception.[5] There have been 621 films nominated for Best Picture and 98 winners.[6]

## History

[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit&section=1 "Edit section: History")]

### Category name changes

[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit&section=2 "Edit section: Category name changes")]

At the [1st Academy Awards](/wiki/1st_Academy_Awards "1st Academy Awards") ceremony held in 1929 (for films made in 1927 and 1928), there were two categories of awards that were each considered the top award of the night: "Outstanding Picture" and "Unique and Artistic Picture", the former being won by the war epic _[Wings](/wiki/Wings_\(1927_film\) "Wings \(1927 film\)")_ , and the latter by the art film _[Sunrise](/wiki/Sunrise:_A_Song_of_Two_Humans "Sunrise: A Song of Two Humans")_. Each award was intended to honor different and equally important aspects of superior filmmaking. In particular, _[The Jazz Singer](/wiki/The_Jazz_Singer "The Jazz Singer")_ was disqualified from both awards, since its use of [synchronized sound](/wiki/Synchronized_sound "Synchronized sound") made the film a _[sui generis](/wiki/Sui_generis "Sui generis")_ item that would have unfairly competed against either category, and the Academy granted the film an honorary award instead.[7]

The following year, the Academy dropped the _Unique and Artistic Picture_ award, deciding retroactively that the award won by _Wings_ was the highest honor that could be awarded, and allowed synchronized sound films to compete for the award.[8] Although the award kept the title _Outstanding Picture_ for the next ceremony, the name underwent several changes over the years, as seen below. Since 1962, the award has been simply called _Best Picture_.[6]

  * [1927/28](/wiki/1st_Academy_Awards "1st Academy Awards")–[1928/29](/wiki/2nd_Academy_Awards "2nd Academy Awards"): Academy Award for Outstanding Picture
  * [1929/30](/wiki/3rd_Academy_Awards "3rd Academy Awards")–[1940](/wiki/13th_Academy_Awards "13th Academy Awards"): Academy Award for Outstanding Production
  * [1941](/wiki/14th_Academy_Awards "14th Academy Awards")–[1943](/wiki/16th_Academy_Awards "16th Academy Awards"): Academy Award for Outstanding Motion Picture
  * [1944](/wiki/17th_Academy_Awards "17th Academy Awards")–[1961](/wiki/34th_Academy_Awards "34th Academy Awards"): Academy Award for Best Motion Picture
  * [1962](/wiki/35th_Academy_Awards "35th Academy Awards")–present: Academy Award for Best Picture



### Recipients

[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit&section=3 "Edit section: Recipients")]

Until 1950, this award was presented to a representative of the production company. That year the protocol was changed so that the award was presented to all credited producers. This rule was modified in 1999 to apply a maximum limit of three producers receiving the award, after the five producers of _[Shakespeare in Love](/wiki/Shakespeare_in_Love "Shakespeare in Love")_ had received the award.[9][10][11]

As of 2020[[update]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit), the "Special Rules for the Best Picture of the Year Award" limit recipients to those who meet two main requirements:[12]

  * Those with [screen credit](/wiki/Motion_picture_credits "Motion picture credits") of "producer" or "produced by", explicitly excluding those with the screen credit "executive producer, co-producer, associate producer, line producer, or produced in association with"
  * those three or fewer producers who have performed the major portion of the producing functions



The rules allow a _bona fide_ team of not more than two people to be considered a single "producer" if the two individuals have had an established producing partnership as determined by the [Producers Guild of America](/wiki/Producers_Guild_of_America "Producers Guild of America") Producing Partnership Panel. Final determination of the qualifying producer nominees for each nominated picture will be made by the Producers Branch Executive Committee, including the right to name any additional qualified producer as a nominee.[12]

The Academy can make exceptions to the limit, as when [Anthony Minghella](/wiki/Anthony_Minghella "Anthony Minghella") and [Sydney Pollack](/wiki/Sydney_Pollack "Sydney Pollack") were posthumously included among the four producers nominated for _[The Reader](/wiki/The_Reader_\(2008_film\) "The Reader \(2008 film\)")_ (2008).[13] As of 2014[[update]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit) the Producers Branch Executive Committee determines such exceptions, noting they take place only in "rare and extraordinary circumstance[s]."[12]

[Steven Spielberg](/wiki/Steven_Spielberg "Steven Spielberg") holds the record for most nominations at fourteen, winning one, while [Kathleen Kennedy](/wiki/Kathleen_Kennedy_\(producer\) "Kathleen Kennedy \(producer\)") holds the record for most nominations without a win at eight. [Sam Spiegel](/wiki/Sam_Spiegel "Sam Spiegel") and [Saul Zaentz](/wiki/Saul_Zaentz "Saul Zaentz") tie for the most wins with three each. During the time when the Oscar was given to production companies instead, [Metro-Goldwyn-Mayer](/wiki/Metro-Goldwyn-Mayer "Metro-Goldwyn-Mayer") received the most, with five wins and 40 nominations. 

### Best Picture and Best Director

[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit&section=4 "Edit section: Best Picture and Best Director")]

The Academy Awards for Best Picture and [Best Director](/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Director "Academy Award for Best Director") have been closely linked throughout their history. Of the 98 films that have won Best Picture, 71 have also been awarded Best Director. Only six films have been awarded Best Picture without receiving a Best Director nomination: _[Wings](/wiki/Wings_\(1927_film\) "Wings \(1927 film\)")_ directed by [William A. Wellman](/wiki/William_A._Wellman "William A. Wellman") (1927/28), _[Grand Hotel](/wiki/Grand_Hotel_\(1932_film\) "Grand Hotel \(1932 film\)")_ directed by [Edmund Goulding](/wiki/Edmund_Goulding "Edmund Goulding") (1931/32), _[Driving Miss Daisy](/wiki/Driving_Miss_Daisy "Driving Miss Daisy")_ directed by [Bruce Beresford](/wiki/Bruce_Beresford "Bruce Beresford") (1989), _[Argo](/wiki/Argo_\(2012_film\) "Argo \(2012 film\)")_ directed by [Ben Affleck](/wiki/Ben_Affleck "Ben Affleck") (2012), _[Green Book](/wiki/Green_Book_\(film\) "Green Book \(film\)")_ directed by [Peter Farrelly](/wiki/Peter_Farrelly "Peter Farrelly") (2018), and _[CODA](/wiki/CODA_\(2021_film\) "CODA \(2021 film\)")_ directed by [Sian Heder](/wiki/Sian_Heder "Sian Heder") (2021). The only two Best Director winners to win for films that did not receive a Best Picture nomination were during the early years of the awards: [Lewis Milestone](/wiki/Lewis_Milestone "Lewis Milestone") for _[Two Arabian Knights](/wiki/Two_Arabian_Knights "Two Arabian Knights")_ (1927/28), and [Frank Lloyd](/wiki/Frank_Lloyd "Frank Lloyd") for _[The Divine Lady](/wiki/The_Divine_Lady "The Divine Lady")_ (1928/29).[14]

### Nomination limit increased

[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit&section=5 "Edit section: Nomination limit increased")]

On June 24, 2009, the [Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Sciences](/wiki/Academy_of_Motion_Picture_Arts_and_Sciences "Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Sciences") (AMPAS) announced that the number of films to be nominated in the Best Picture award category would increase from 5 to 10, starting with the [82nd Academy Awards](/wiki/82nd_Academy_Awards "82nd Academy Awards") (2009).[15] Although the Academy never officially said so, many commenters noted the expansion was likely in part a response to public criticism of _[The Dark Knight](/wiki/The_Dark_Knight "The Dark Knight")_ and _[WALL-E](/wiki/WALL-E "WALL-E")_ (both 2008) (and, in previous years, other blockbusters and popular films) not being nominated for Best Picture.[16][17][18] Officially, the Academy said the rule change was a throwback to the Academy's early years in the 1930s and 1940s, when 8 to 12 films were nominated each year. "Having 10 Best Picture nominees is going to allow Academy voters to recognize and include some of the fantastic movies that often show up in the other Oscar categories but have been squeezed out of the race for the top prize," AMPAS President [Sid Ganis](/wiki/Sid_Ganis "Sid Ganis") said in a press conference. "I can't wait to see what that list of 10 looks like when the nominees are announced in February."[15]

At the same time, the voting system was switched from [first-past-the-post](/wiki/First-past-the-post "First-past-the-post") to [instant runoff voting](/wiki/Instant-runoff_voting "Instant-runoff voting") (also known as preferential voting).[19] In 2011, the Academy revised the rule again so that the number of films nominated was between 5 and 10; nominated films must earn either 5% of first-place rankings or 5% after an abbreviated variation of the [single transferable vote](/wiki/Single_transferable_vote "Single transferable vote") nominating process.[20] Bruce Davis, the Academy executive director at the time, said, "A Best Picture nomination should be an indication of extraordinary merit. If there are only eight pictures that truly earn that honor in a given year, we shouldn't feel an obligation to round out the number."[21] This system lasted until 2021, when the Academy reverted back to a set number of ten nominees from the [94th Academy Awards](/wiki/94th_Academy_Awards "94th Academy Awards") onward.[22]

### Language and country of origin

[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit&section=6 "Edit section: Language and country of origin")]

Twenty-one non-[English language](/wiki/English_language "English language") films have been nominated in the category: _[La Grande Illusion](/wiki/La_Grande_Illusion "La Grande Illusion")_ (French, 1938); _[Z](/wiki/Z_\(1969_film\) "Z \(1969 film\)")_ (French, 1969); _[The Emigrants](/wiki/The_Emigrants_\(film\) "The Emigrants \(film\)")_ (Swedish, 1972); _[Cries and Whispers](/wiki/Cries_and_Whispers "Cries and Whispers")_ (Swedish, 1973); _[The Postman (Il Postino)](/wiki/Il_Postino:_The_Postman "Il Postino: The Postman")_ (Italian/Spanish, 1995); _[Life Is Beautiful](/wiki/Life_Is_Beautiful "Life Is Beautiful")_ (Italian, 1998); _[Crouching Tiger, Hidden Dragon](/wiki/Crouching_Tiger,_Hidden_Dragon "Crouching Tiger, Hidden Dragon")_ ([Mandarin Chinese](/wiki/Standard_Chinese "Standard Chinese"), 2000); _[Letters from Iwo Jima](/wiki/Letters_from_Iwo_Jima "Letters from Iwo Jima")_ (Japanese, 2006, but ineligible for [Best Foreign Language Film](/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_International_Feature_Film "Academy Award for Best International Feature Film") because it was an American production); _[Amour](/wiki/Amour_\(2012_film\) "Amour \(2012 film\)")_ (French, 2012); [_Roma_](/wiki/Roma_\(2018_film\) "Roma \(2018 film\)") (Spanish/Mixtec, 2018); _[Parasite](/wiki/Parasite_\(2019_film\) "Parasite \(2019 film\)")_ (Korean, 2019); _[Minari](/wiki/Minari_\(film\) "Minari \(film\)")_ (Korean, 2020, but ineligible for Best International Feature Film because it was an American production);[23] _[Drive My Car](/wiki/Drive_My_Car_\(film\) "Drive My Car \(film\)")_ (Japanese/Korean/Mandarin Chinese/German/[Korean Sign Language](/wiki/Korean_Sign_Language "Korean Sign Language"), 2021), _[All Quiet on the Western Front](/wiki/All_Quiet_on_the_Western_Front_\(2022_film\) "All Quiet on the Western Front \(2022 film\)")_ (German, 2022), _[Anatomy of a Fall](/wiki/Anatomy_of_a_Fall "Anatomy of a Fall")_ (French, 2023), _[Past Lives](/wiki/Past_Lives_\(film\) "Past Lives \(film\)")_ (Korean, 2023, but ineligible for Best International Feature Film because it was an American production), _[The Zone of Interest](/wiki/The_Zone_of_Interest_\(film\) "The Zone of Interest \(film\)")_ (German/Polish/Yiddish, 2023), _[Emilia Pérez](/wiki/Emilia_P%C3%A9rez "Emilia Pérez")_ (Spanish, 2024), _[I'm Still Here](/wiki/I%27m_Still_Here_\(2024_film\) "I'm Still Here \(2024 film\)")_ (Portuguese, 2024), _[The Secret Agent](/wiki/The_Secret_Agent_\(2025_film\) "The Secret Agent \(2025 film\)")_ (Portuguese, 2025), and _[Sentimental Value](/wiki/Sentimental_Value "Sentimental Value")_ (Norwegian, 2025). _Parasite_ became the first film not in English to win Best Picture.[24][25]

Ten films wholly financed outside the United States have won Best Picture, eight of which were financed, in part or in whole, by the United Kingdom: _[Hamlet](/wiki/Hamlet_\(1948_film\) "Hamlet \(1948 film\)")_ (1948), _[Tom Jones](/wiki/Tom_Jones_\(1963_film\) "Tom Jones \(1963 film\)")_ (1963), _[A Man for All Seasons](/wiki/A_Man_for_All_Seasons_\(1966_film\) "A Man for All Seasons \(1966 film\)")_ (1966), _[Chariots of Fire](/wiki/Chariots_of_Fire "Chariots of Fire")_ (1981), _[Gandhi](/wiki/Gandhi_\(film\) "Gandhi \(film\)")_ (1982), _[The Last Emperor](/wiki/The_Last_Emperor "The Last Emperor")_ (1987), _[Slumdog Millionaire](/wiki/Slumdog_Millionaire "Slumdog Millionaire")_ (2008), and _[The King's Speech](/wiki/The_King%27s_Speech "The King's Speech")_ (2010). The ninth film, _[The Artist](/wiki/The_Artist_\(film\) "The Artist \(film\)")_ (2011), was financed in France, and the tenth film, _[Parasite](/wiki/Parasite_\(2019_film\) "Parasite \(2019 film\)")_ (2019), was financed in South Korea.[26]

### Rating

[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit&section=7 "Edit section: Rating")]

Since 1968, most Best Picture winners have been rated R under the [Motion Picture Association](/wiki/Motion_Picture_Association "Motion Picture Association")'s [rating system](/wiki/Motion_Picture_Association_film_rating_system "Motion Picture Association film rating system"). _[Oliver!](/wiki/Oliver!_\(film\) "Oliver! \(film\)")_ is the only G-rated film and _[Midnight Cowboy](/wiki/Midnight_Cowboy "Midnight Cowboy")_ is the only X-rated film (what is categorized as an NC-17 film today), so far, to win Best Picture; they won in back-to-back years, 1968 and 1969. The latter has since been changed to an R rating. Eleven films have won with a PG rating: the first was _[Patton](/wiki/Patton_\(film\) "Patton \(film\)")_ (1970) and the most recent was _[Driving Miss Daisy](/wiki/Driving_Miss_Daisy "Driving Miss Daisy")_ (1989). Eleven more films have won with a PG-13 rating (which was introduced in 1984): the first was _[The Last Emperor](/wiki/The_Last_Emperor "The Last Emperor")_ (1987) and the most recent was _[CODA](/wiki/CODA_\(2021_film\) "CODA \(2021 film\)")_ (2021). For unrated films, _[A Room with a View](/wiki/A_Room_with_a_View_\(1985_film\) "A Room with a View \(1985 film\)")_ (1985) is the first film to not be rated by the MPA and be nominated for Best Picture, though no unrated films have won Best Picture. 

### Genres and mediums

[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit&section=8 "Edit section: Genres and mediums")]

_Find sources:_ ["Academy Award for Best Picture"](https://www.google.com/search?as_eq=wikipedia&q=%22Academy+Award+for+Best+Picture%22) – [news](https://www.google.com/search?tbm=nws&q=%22Academy+Award+for+Best+Picture%22+-wikipedia&tbs=ar:1) **·** [newspapers](https://www.google.com/search?&q=%22Academy+Award+for+Best+Picture%22&tbs=bkt:s&tbm=bks) **·** [books](https://www.google.com/search?tbs=bks:1&q=%22Academy+Award+for+Best+Picture%22+-wikipedia) **·** [scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar?q=%22Academy+Award+for+Best+Picture%22) **·** [JSTOR](https://www.jstor.org/action/doBasicSearch?Query=%22Academy+Award+for+Best+Picture%22&acc=on&wc=on) _( September 2023)__([Learn how and when to remove this message](/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal "Help:Maintenance template removal"))_  
---|---  
  
Three animated films have been nominated for Best Picture: _[Beauty and the Beast](/wiki/Beauty_and_the_Beast_\(1991_film\) "Beauty and the Beast \(1991 film\)")_ (1991), _[Up](/wiki/Up_\(2009_film\) "Up \(2009 film\)")_ (2009), and _[Toy Story 3](/wiki/Toy_Story_3 "Toy Story 3")_ (2010). The latter two were nominated after the Academy expanded the number of nominees, but none have won. 

No comic book film has won, although three have been nominated: _[Skippy](/wiki/Skippy_\(film\) "Skippy \(film\)")_ (1931), _[Black Panther](/wiki/Black_Panther_\(film\) "Black Panther \(film\)")_ (2018), and _[Joker](/wiki/Joker_\(2019_film\) "Joker \(2019 film\)")_ (2019).[27]

Two fantasy films have won: _[The Lord of the Rings: The Return of the King](/wiki/The_Lord_of_the_Rings:_The_Return_of_the_King "The Lord of the Rings: The Return of the King")_ (2003) and _[The Shape of Water](/wiki/The_Shape_of_Water "The Shape of Water")_ (2017), although more have been nominated. 

_[The Silence of the Lambs](/wiki/The_Silence_of_the_Lambs_\(film\) "The Silence of the Lambs \(film\)")_ (1991) is the only horror/thriller film to win Best Picture. Eight others have been nominated: _[The Exorcist](/wiki/The_Exorcist "The Exorcist")_ (1973), _[Jaws](/wiki/Jaws_\(film\) "Jaws \(film\)")_ (1975), _[The Sixth Sense](/wiki/The_Sixth_Sense "The Sixth Sense")_ (1999), _[Black Swan](/wiki/Black_Swan_\(film\) "Black Swan \(film\)")_ (2010), _[Get Out](/wiki/Get_Out "Get Out")_ (2017), _[The Substance](/wiki/The_Substance "The Substance")_ (2024), _[Frankenstein](/wiki/Frankenstein_\(2025_film\) "Frankenstein \(2025 film\)")_ (2025), and _[Sinners](/wiki/Sinners_\(2025_film\) "Sinners \(2025 film\)")_ (2025). 

Several science-fiction films have been nominated for Best Picture, though _[Everything Everywhere All at Once](/wiki/Everything_Everywhere_All_at_Once "Everything Everywhere All at Once")_ (2022) was the first one to win.[28]

_[Titanic](/wiki/Titanic_\(1997_film\) "Titanic \(1997 film\)")_ (1997) is the only [disaster film](/wiki/Disaster_film "Disaster film") to win Best Picture, though other such films have been nominated, including _[Airport](/wiki/Airport_\(1970_film\) "Airport \(1970 film\)")_ (1970) and _[The Towering Inferno](/wiki/The_Towering_Inferno "The Towering Inferno")_ (1974). 

No documentary has been nominated for Best Picture, although _[Chang: A Drama of the Wilderness](/wiki/Chang:_A_Drama_of_the_Wilderness "Chang: A Drama of the Wilderness")_ was nominated in the _Unique and Artistic Picture_ category at the 1927/28 awards. A [Best Documentary Feature](/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Documentary_Feature_Film "Academy Award for Best Documentary Feature Film") category was introduced in 1941. 

Several musical adaptations based on material previously filmed in non-musical form have won Best Picture, including _[Gigi](/wiki/Gigi_\(1958_film\) "Gigi \(1958 film\)")_ , _[West Side Story](/wiki/West_Side_Story_\(1961_film\) "West Side Story \(1961 film\)")_ , _[My Fair Lady](/wiki/My_Fair_Lady_\(film\) "My Fair Lady \(film\)")_ , _[The Sound of Music](/wiki/The_Sound_of_Music_\(film\) "The Sound of Music \(film\)")_ , _[Oliver!](/wiki/Oliver!_\(film\) "Oliver! \(film\)")_ , and _[Chicago](/wiki/Chicago_\(2002_film\) "Chicago \(2002 film\)")_. 

Several [epics](/wiki/Epic_film "Epic film") or historical epic films have won Best Picture, including the first recipient _[Wings](/wiki/Wings_\(1927_film\) "Wings \(1927 film\)")_. Others include _[Cimarron](/wiki/Cimarron_\(1931_film\) "Cimarron \(1931 film\)")_ , _[Cavalcade](/wiki/Cavalcade_\(1933_film\) "Cavalcade \(1933 film\)")_ , _[Gone with the Wind](/wiki/Gone_with_the_Wind_\(film\) "Gone with the Wind \(film\)")_ , _[The Bridge on the River Kwai](/wiki/The_Bridge_on_the_River_Kwai "The Bridge on the River Kwai")_ , _[Ben-Hur](/wiki/Ben-Hur_\(1959_film\) "Ben-Hur \(1959 film\)")_ , _[Lawrence of Arabia](/wiki/Lawrence_of_Arabia_\(film\) "Lawrence of Arabia \(film\)")_ , _[Patton](/wiki/Patton_\(film\) "Patton \(film\)")_ , _[The Godfather](/wiki/The_Godfather "The Godfather")_ , _[The Godfather Part II](/wiki/The_Godfather_Part_II "The Godfather Part II")_ , _[Gandhi](/wiki/Gandhi_\(film\) "Gandhi \(film\)")_ , _[The Last Emperor](/wiki/The_Last_Emperor "The Last Emperor")_ , _[Dances With Wolves](/wiki/Dances_With_Wolves "Dances With Wolves")_ , _[Schindler's List](/wiki/Schindler%27s_List "Schindler's List")_ , _[Forrest Gump](/wiki/Forrest_Gump "Forrest Gump")_ , _[Braveheart](/wiki/Braveheart "Braveheart")_ , _[The English Patient](/wiki/The_English_Patient_\(film\) "The English Patient \(film\)")_ , _[Titanic](/wiki/Titanic_\(1997_film\) "Titanic \(1997 film\)")_ , _[Gladiator](/wiki/Gladiator_\(2000_film\) "Gladiator \(2000 film\)")_ , _[The Lord of the Rings: The Return of the King](/wiki/The_Lord_of_the_Rings:_The_Return_of_the_King "The Lord of the Rings: The Return of the King")_ , and _[Oppenheimer](/wiki/Oppenheimer_\(film\) "Oppenheimer \(film\)")_. 

Several [war films](/wiki/War_film "War film") have been nominated for Best Picture, with _[Wings](/wiki/Wings_\(1927_film\) "Wings \(1927 film\)")_ , _[All Quiet on the Western Front](/wiki/All_Quiet_on_the_Western_Front_\(1930_film\) "All Quiet on the Western Front \(1930 film\)")_ , _[From Here to Eternity](/wiki/From_Here_to_Eternity "From Here to Eternity")_ , _[The Bridge on the River Kwai](/wiki/The_Bridge_on_the_River_Kwai "The Bridge on the River Kwai")_ , _[Lawrence of Arabia](/wiki/Lawrence_of_Arabia_\(film\) "Lawrence of Arabia \(film\)")_ , _[Patton](/wiki/Patton_\(film\) "Patton \(film\)")_ , _[Platoon](/wiki/Platoon_\(film\) "Platoon \(film\)")_ , and _[The Hurt Locker](/wiki/The_Hurt_Locker "The Hurt Locker")_ being some of the many winners. 

### Sequel nominations and winners

[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit&section=9 "Edit section: Sequel nominations and winners")]

Ten films that were presented as direct sequels have been nominated for Best Picture: _[The Bells of St. Mary's](/wiki/The_Bells_of_St._Mary%27s "The Bells of St. Mary's")_ (1945; the sequel to the 1944 winner, _[Going My Way](/wiki/Going_My_Way "Going My Way")_), _[The Godfather Part II](/wiki/The_Godfather_Part_II "The Godfather Part II")_ (1974), _[The Godfather Part III](/wiki/The_Godfather_Part_III "The Godfather Part III")_ (1990), _[The Lord of the Rings: The Two Towers](/wiki/The_Lord_of_the_Rings:_The_Two_Towers "The Lord of the Rings: The Two Towers")_ (2002), _[The Lord of the Rings: The Return of the King](/wiki/The_Lord_of_the_Rings:_The_Return_of_the_King "The Lord of the Rings: The Return of the King")_ (2003), _[Toy Story 3](/wiki/Toy_Story_3 "Toy Story 3")_ (2010), _[Mad Max: Fury Road](/wiki/Mad_Max:_Fury_Road "Mad Max: Fury Road")_ (2015)_,[Avatar: The Way of Water](/wiki/Avatar:_The_Way_of_Water "Avatar: The Way of Water")_ (2022), _[Top Gun: Maverick](/wiki/Top_Gun:_Maverick "Top Gun: Maverick")_ (2022), and _[Dune: Part Two](/wiki/Dune:_Part_Two "Dune: Part Two")_ (2024). 

_Toy Story 3_ , _Mad Max: Fury Road_ , and _Top Gun: Maverick_ are the only sequels to be nominated without any predecessors being nominated. _The Godfather Part II_ and _The Lord of the Rings: The Return of the King_ are the only sequels to have won the award, and their respective trilogies are the only series to have three films nominated. [_The Godfather_ series](/wiki/The_Godfather_\(film_series\) "The Godfather \(film series\)") is the only film series with multiple Best Picture winners, with the [first film](/wiki/The_Godfather "The Godfather") winning the award for 1972 and the [second film](/wiki/The_Godfather_Part_II "The Godfather Part II") winning the award for 1974.[23]

Another nominee, _[Broadway Melody of 1936](/wiki/Broadway_Melody_of_1936 "Broadway Melody of 1936")_ , was a follow-up of sorts to previous winner _[The Broadway Melody](/wiki/The_Broadway_Melody "The Broadway Melody")_ , but beyond the title and some music, the two films have mutually independent stories. _[The Silence of the Lambs](/wiki/The_Silence_of_the_Lambs_\(film\) "The Silence of the Lambs \(film\)")_ was adapted from the sequel novel to _[Red Dragon](/wiki/Red_Dragon_\(novel\) "Red Dragon \(novel\)")_ , which had been adapted for film as _[Manhunter](/wiki/Manhunter_\(film\) "Manhunter \(film\)")_ by a different studio, but the two films have different casts and creative teams and were not presented as a series; [an adaptation](/wiki/Red_Dragon_\(2002_film\) "Red Dragon \(2002 film\)") of _Red Dragon_ in the same continuity as _The Silence of the Lambs_ was later produced.[29] Conversely, 2024's _[Wicked](/wiki/Wicked_\(2024_film\) "Wicked \(2024 film\)")_ uses iconography and characters who appeared in 1939's _[The Wizard of Oz](/wiki/The_Wizard_of_Oz "The Wizard of Oz")_ and other _Oz_ films, but is not a direct prequel to any film. 

_[The Lion in Winter](/wiki/The_Lion_in_Winter_\(1968_film\) "The Lion in Winter \(1968 film\)")_ features [Peter O'Toole](/wiki/Peter_O%27Toole "Peter O'Toole") as [King Henry II](/wiki/Henry_II_of_England "Henry II of England"), a role he had played previously in the film _[Becket](/wiki/Becket_\(1964_film\) "Becket \(1964 film\)")_ , but _The Lion in Winter_ is not a sequel to _Becket_. Similarly, _[The Queen](/wiki/The_Queen_\(2006_film\) "The Queen \(2006 film\)")_ features [Michael Sheen](/wiki/Michael_Sheen "Michael Sheen") as [Tony Blair](/wiki/Tony_Blair "Tony Blair"), a role he had played previously in the television film _[The Deal](/wiki/The_Deal_\(2003_film\) "The Deal \(2003 film\)")_. [Christine Langan](/wiki/Christine_Langan "Christine Langan"), producer of both productions, described _The Queen_ as not being a direct sequel, only that it reunited the same creative team.[30]

[Clint Eastwood](/wiki/Clint_Eastwood "Clint Eastwood")'s _[Letters from Iwo Jima](/wiki/Letters_from_Iwo_Jima "Letters from Iwo Jima")_ was a companion piece to his film _[Flags of Our Fathers](/wiki/Flags_of_Our_Fathers_\(film\) "Flags of Our Fathers \(film\)")_ that was released earlier the same year. These two films depict the same battle from the different viewpoints of Japanese and United States military forces; the two films were shot back-to-back. 

In addition, _[Black Panther](/wiki/Black_Panther_\(film\) "Black Panther \(film\)")_ is a continuation of the events that occurred in _[Captain America: Civil War](/wiki/Captain_America:_Civil_War "Captain America: Civil War")_ and the [Marvel Cinematic Universe](/wiki/Marvel_Cinematic_Universe "Marvel Cinematic Universe"). 

### Remake nominations and winners

[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit&section=10 "Edit section: Remake nominations and winners")]

Along similar lines to sequels, there have been few nominees and winners that are either remakes or adaptations of the same source materials or subjects. 

_[Ben-Hur](/wiki/Ben-Hur_\(1959_film\) "Ben-Hur \(1959 film\)")_ , which won Best Picture of 1959, is a remake of the [1925 silent film with a similar title](/wiki/Ben-Hur:_A_Tale_of_the_Christ_\(1925_film\) "Ben-Hur: A Tale of the Christ \(1925 film\)") and both were adapted from [Lew Wallace](/wiki/Lew_Wallace "Lew Wallace")'s 1880 novel _[Ben-Hur: A Tale of the Christ](/wiki/Ben-Hur:_A_Tale_of_the_Christ "Ben-Hur: A Tale of the Christ")_. _[The Departed](/wiki/The_Departed "The Departed")_ , which won Best Picture of 2006, is a remake of the 2002 [Hong Kong](/wiki/Hong_Kong_cinema "Hong Kong cinema") film _[Infernal Affairs](/wiki/Infernal_Affairs "Infernal Affairs")_ and is the first remake of a non-English language or international film to win. 

Other nominees include 1963's _[Cleopatra](/wiki/Cleopatra_\(1963_film\) "Cleopatra \(1963 film\)")_ about the [titular last queen of Egypt](/wiki/Cleopatra "Cleopatra") following the [1934 version](/wiki/Cleopatra_\(1934_film\) "Cleopatra \(1934 film\)"), 2018's _[A Star is Born](/wiki/A_Star_Is_Born_\(2018_film\) "A Star Is Born \(2018 film\)")_ following the [1937 film of the same name](/wiki/A_Star_Is_Born_\(1937_film\) "A Star Is Born \(1937 film\)"), and 2019's _[Little Women](/wiki/Little_Women_\(2019_film\) "Little Women \(2019 film\)")_ following the [1933 film of the same name](/wiki/Little_Women_\(1933_film\) "Little Women \(1933 film\)") with both being adaptations of the [1868 novel](/wiki/Little_Women "Little Women").[31] _[True Grit](/wiki/True_Grit_\(2010_film\) "True Grit \(2010 film\)")_ , which was nominated for Best Picture at the [83rd Academy Awards](/wiki/83rd_Academy_Awards "83rd Academy Awards"), is the second adaptation of [Charles Portis](/wiki/Charles_Portis "Charles Portis")'s [1968 novel](/wiki/True_Grit_\(novel\) "True Grit \(novel\)") following the [1969 film of the same name](/wiki/True_Grit_\(1969_film\) "True Grit \(1969 film\)"). 

Four of the nominees for the [94th ceremony](/wiki/94th_Academy_Awards "94th Academy Awards") were based on source material previously made into films: _[CODA](/wiki/CODA_\(2021_film\) "CODA \(2021 film\)")_ , _[Dune](/wiki/Dune_\(2021_film\) "Dune \(2021 film\)")_ , _[Nightmare Alley](/wiki/Nightmare_Alley_\(2021_film\) "Nightmare Alley \(2021 film\)")_ , and _[West Side Story](/wiki/West_Side_Story_\(2021_film\) "West Side Story \(2021 film\)")_. The 2021 version of _West Side Story_ became the second adaptation of the same source material for a previous Best Picture winner to be nominated for the same award after 1962's _[Mutiny on the Bounty](/wiki/Mutiny_on_the_Bounty_\(1962_film\) "Mutiny on the Bounty \(1962 film\)")_.[32] For that same ceremony, _CODA_ became the second remake of a non-English-language or international film to win. 

The 2022 German-language _[All Quiet on the Western Front](/wiki/All_Quiet_on_the_Western_Front_\(2022_film\) "All Quiet on the Western Front \(2022 film\)")_ is the second adaptation of the [1929 novel](/wiki/All_Quiet_on_the_Western_Front "All Quiet on the Western Front") after the [1930 English-language film](/wiki/All_Quiet_on_the_Western_Front_\(1930_film\) "All Quiet on the Western Front \(1930 film\)"), and the third adaptation of the same source material of a previous Best Picture winner.[33]

### Silent film winners

[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit&section=11 "Edit section: Silent film winners")]

_Wings_ (1927), winner of the first Oscar for Best Picture

At the [1st Academy Awards](/wiki/1st_Academy_Awards "1st Academy Awards"), the Best Picture award (then named "Academy Award for Outstanding Picture") was presented to the 1927 [silent film](/wiki/Silent_film "Silent film") _[Wings](/wiki/Wings_\(1927_film\) "Wings \(1927 film\)")_. 

_[The Artist](/wiki/The_Artist_\(film\) "The Artist \(film\)")_ (2011) was the first essentially silent (with the exception of a single scene of dialogue, and a dream sequence with sound effects) film since _Wings_ to win Best Picture. It was the first silent nominee since 1928's _[The Patriot](/wiki/The_Patriot_\(1928_film\) "The Patriot \(1928 film\)")_ and the first Best Picture winner to be produced entirely in [black-and-white](/wiki/Black-and-white "Black-and-white") since 1960's _[The Apartment](/wiki/The_Apartment "The Apartment")_. (_[Schindler's List](/wiki/Schindler%27s_List "Schindler's List")_ , the 1993 winner, was predominantly black-and-white but contains some color sequences.)[26]

### Version availability

[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit&section=12 "Edit section: Version availability")]

No Best Picture winner has been [lost](/wiki/Lost_film "Lost film"), though a few such as _[All Quiet on the Western Front](/wiki/All_Quiet_on_the_Western_Front_\(1930_film\) "All Quiet on the Western Front \(1930 film\)")_ and _[Lawrence of Arabia](/wiki/Lawrence_of_Arabia_\(film\) "Lawrence of Arabia \(film\)")_ exist only in a form altered from their original, award-winning release form. This has usually been due to editing for reissue (and subsequently partly restored by archivists). Other winners and nominees, such as _[Tom Jones](/wiki/Tom_Jones_\(1963_film\) "Tom Jones \(1963 film\)")_ (prior to its 2018 reissues by [The Criterion Collection](/wiki/The_Criterion_Collection "The Criterion Collection") and the [British Film Institute](/wiki/British_Film_Institute "British Film Institute")) and _[Star Wars](/wiki/Star_Wars_\(film\) "Star Wars \(film\)")_ , are widely available only in subsequently altered versions. _[The Broadway Melody](/wiki/The_Broadway_Melody "The Broadway Melody")_ originally had some sequences photographed in [two-color Technicolor](/wiki/Technicolor#Process_3 "Technicolor"). This footage survives only in black and white.[34]

The 1928 film _[The Patriot](/wiki/The_Patriot_\(1928_film\) "The Patriot \(1928 film\)")_ is the only Best Picture nominee that is lost (about one-third is extant).[35] _[The Racket](/wiki/The_Racket_\(1928_film\) "The Racket \(1928 film\)")_ , also from 1928, was believed lost for many years until a print was found in [Howard Hughes](/wiki/Howard_Hughes "Howard Hughes")' archives. It has since been restored and shown on [Turner Classic Movies](/wiki/Turner_Classic_Movies "Turner Classic Movies").[36] The only surviving complete prints of 1931's _[East Lynne](/wiki/East_Lynne_\(1931_film\) "East Lynne \(1931 film\)")_ and 1934's _[The White Parade](/wiki/The_White_Parade "The White Parade")_ exist within the [UCLA](/wiki/UCLA "UCLA") film archive.[37]

### Diversity standards

[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit&section=13 "Edit section: Diversity standards")]

The Academy has established a set of "representation and inclusion standards", called Academy Aperture 2025, which a film is now required to satisfy in order to compete in the Best Picture category, starting with the [96th Academy Awards](/wiki/96th_Academy_Awards "96th Academy Awards") for films released in 2023.[38][39] There are four general standards, of which a film must satisfy two to be considered for Best Picture: (a) on-screen representation, themes and narratives; (b) creative leadership and project team; (c) industry access and opportunities; and (d) audience development.[38] As explained by [Vox](/wiki/Vox_\(website\) "Vox \(website\)"), the standards "basically break down into two big buckets: standards promoting more inclusive representation and standards promoting more inclusive employment".[40] The standards are intended to provide greater opportunities for employment, in cast, crew, studio apprenticeships and internships, and development, marketing, publicity, and distribution executives, among underrepresented racial and ethnic groups, women, LGBTQ+ people, and persons with [cognitive](/wiki/Disabilities_affecting_intellectual_abilities "Disabilities affecting intellectual abilities") or [physical disabilities](/wiki/Physical_disability "Physical disability"), or who are [deaf or hard of hearing](/wiki/Deafness "Deafness").[38][41]

These standards only apply to the Best Picture category and do not affect a film's eligibility in other Oscar categories.[38] For the [94th](/wiki/94th_Academy_Awards "94th Academy Awards") and [95th Academy Awards](/wiki/95th_Academy_Awards "95th Academy Awards") (films released in 2021 and 2022), filmmakers were required to submit a confidential Academy Inclusion Standards form to be considered for Best Picture but were not required to fulfill the standards.[40]

### 2017 ceremony mistake

[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit&section=14 "Edit section: 2017 ceremony mistake")]

At the [89th Academy Awards](/wiki/89th_Academy_Awards "89th Academy Awards") on February 26, 2017, presenter [Faye Dunaway](/wiki/Faye_Dunaway "Faye Dunaway") read _[La La Land](/wiki/La_La_Land "La La Land")_ as the winner of the award. However, she and [Warren Beatty](/wiki/Warren_Beatty "Warren Beatty") had mistakenly been given the duplicate envelope for the "[Best Actress in a Leading Role](/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Actress "Academy Award for Best Actress")" award, which [Emma Stone](/wiki/Emma_Stone "Emma Stone") had won for her role in _La La Land_. While accepting the award, _La La Land_ producer [Jordan Horowitz](/wiki/Jordan_Horowitz "Jordan Horowitz"), who was given the correct envelope, realized the mistake and announced that _[Moonlight](/wiki/Moonlight_\(2016_film\) "Moonlight \(2016 film\)")_ had won the award.[42]

## Criticisms and controversies

[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit&section=15 "Edit section: Criticisms and controversies")]

### _High Noon_ and _The Greatest Show on Earth_

[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit&section=16 "Edit section: High Noon and The Greatest Show on Earth")]

Further information: [The Greatest Show on Earth (film) § Oscar controversy](/wiki/The_Greatest_Show_on_Earth_\(film\)#Oscar_controversy "The Greatest Show on Earth \(film\)")

The selection of _[The Greatest Show on Earth](/wiki/The_Greatest_Show_on_Earth_\(film\) "The Greatest Show on Earth \(film\)")_ rather than _[High Noon](/wiki/High_Noon "High Noon")_ at the [25th Academy Awards](/wiki/25th_Academy_Awards "25th Academy Awards") was listed by _[Time](/wiki/Time_\(magazine\) "Time \(magazine\)")_ among the 10 most controversial Best Picture races.[43] Retrospectively, _The Greatest Show on Earth_ has been considered by some to be one of the worst Best Picture winners in history.[44]

### Diversity criticisms

[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit&section=17 "Edit section: Diversity criticisms")]

Further information: [Brokeback Mountain § Best Picture controversy](/wiki/Brokeback_Mountain#Best_Picture_controversy "Brokeback Mountain"), [Crash (2004 film) § Oscar controversy](/wiki/Crash_\(2004_film\)#Oscar_controversy "Crash \(2004 film\)"), and [Green Book (film) § Criticism from Shirley's relatives](/wiki/Green_Book_\(film\)#Criticism_from_Shirley's_relatives "Green Book \(film\)")

In general, the awardees of that category have been criticized for disproportionately recognizing films about white men over those of women or non-white people.[45] In opposition, the Academy's decision to favor Best Picture winning films with depiction of race relations among people of color—most primarily _[Driving Miss Daisy](/wiki/Driving_Miss_Daisy "Driving Miss Daisy")_ , _[Crash](/wiki/Crash_\(2004_film\) "Crash \(2004 film\)")_ , and _[Green Book](/wiki/Green_Book_\(film\) "Green Book \(film\)")_ , all of which were directed by white filmmakers—led to significant backlash over racism against the Academy.[46]

In 2005, _[Brokeback Mountain](/wiki/Brokeback_Mountain "Brokeback Mountain")_ losing the Best Picture to _[Crash](/wiki/Crash_\(2004_film\) "Crash \(2004 film\)")_ was heavily criticized, with some critics such as [Kenneth Turan](/wiki/Kenneth_Turan "Kenneth Turan") accusing the Academy members of homophobia and benefitting from making a non-groundbreaking choice in _Crash_ ,[47][48] considered as one of the most notable Oscars upsets.[49][50][51] After announcing the award, presenter [Jack Nicholson](/wiki/Jack_Nicholson "Jack Nicholson") was caught on camera mouthing the word "whoa" out of apparent surprise at the result.[52] The film's use of moral quandary as a storytelling medium was widely reported as ironic, since many saw it as the "safe" alternative to _Brokeback Mountain_ , which is about a [gay](/wiki/Gay "Gay") relationship (the other nominees, _[Good Night, and Good Luck](/wiki/Good_Night,_and_Good_Luck "Good Night, and Good Luck")_ , _[Capote](/wiki/Capote_\(film\) "Capote \(film\)")_ , and _[Munich](/wiki/Munich_\(2005_film\) "Munich \(2005 film\)")_ also tackle heavy subjects of [McCarthyism](/wiki/McCarthyism "McCarthyism"), homosexuality, and [terrorism](/wiki/Terrorism "Terrorism"), respectively).[53]

Though there have been exceptions like [Barry Jenkins](/wiki/Barry_Jenkins "Barry Jenkins")'s _[Moonlight](/wiki/Moonlight_\(2016_film\) "Moonlight \(2016 film\)")_ , films like _[Precious](/wiki/Precious_\(film\) "Precious \(film\)")_ and _[Get Out](/wiki/Get_Out "Get Out")_ have been seen as potentially being shut out of the Best Picture race because of older and white Academy voters choosing not to see them.[54] From 2018 onwards, the Academy made an effort to add more younger, female, non-white, and non-American voters, and to create a non-voting "emeritus" status for people who had not worked in the film industry after a certain length of time, in order to diversify and rejuvenate their voter bloc. 

### _Saving Private Ryan_ and _Shakespeare in Love_

[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit&section=18 "Edit section: Saving Private Ryan and Shakespeare in Love")]

Further information: [Saving Private Ryan § Accolades](/wiki/Saving_Private_Ryan#Accolades "Saving Private Ryan"), and [Shakespeare in Love § Oscar controversy](/wiki/Shakespeare_in_Love#Oscar_controversy "Shakespeare in Love")

[](/wiki/File:Harvey_Weinstein_C%C3%A9sars_2014_\(cropped\)_\(centered\).jpg)Weinstein (pictured in 2014).

_[Saving Private Ryan](/wiki/Saving_Private_Ryan "Saving Private Ryan")_ was immediately pegged as a favorite for the category by many members and fans of Spielberg's films, but it lost to _[Shakespeare in Love](/wiki/Shakespeare_in_Love "Shakespeare in Love")._ The Academy's decision was widely criticized.[55][56][57] The choice was seen as one of the biggest upsets in the award's history, and led to [DreamWorks](/wiki/DreamWorks_Pictures "DreamWorks Pictures") executives (including Terry Press) and many industry pundits accusing [Miramax Films](/wiki/Miramax "Miramax") and one of the _Shakespeare in Love_ producers, [Harvey Weinstein](/wiki/Harvey_Weinstein "Harvey Weinstein"), of winning due to their award campaign's negative messaging against _Saving Private Ryan_ rather than their own film's merits. Press stated that Weinstein and Miramax "tried to get everybody to believe that _Saving Private Ryan_ was all in the first 15 minutes".[58][59]

### Animated films in Best Picture category

[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit&section=19 "Edit section: Animated films in Best Picture category")]

[](/wiki/File:Annie_Awards_Jeffrey_Katzenberg.jpg)Katzenberg (pictured in 2006) was responsible for _[Beauty and The Beast](/wiki/Beauty_and_the_Beast_\(1991_film\) "Beauty and the Beast \(1991 film\)")_ being nominated in the Best Picture category.

The category of [Best Animated Feature](/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Animated_Feature "Academy Award for Best Animated Feature") was created for the [74th Academy Awards](/wiki/74th_Academy_Awards "74th Academy Awards") to ensure the recognition of animated films; prior to its creation, the only animated film ever nominated for Best Picture was 1991's _[Beauty and the Beast](/wiki/Beauty_and_the_Beast_\(1991_film\) "Beauty and the Beast \(1991 film\)")._ However, the award has since received criticism on the grounds that it discourages animated films from being eligible to win Best Picture. While the Academy rules allow for a film to be nominated in both categories,[60] only two animated films (_[Up](/wiki/Up_\(2009_film\) "Up \(2009 film\)")_ and _[Toy Story 3](/wiki/Toy_Story_3 "Toy Story 3")_) have been nominated for Best Picture since the creation of the two categories. 

A prominent example was the 2001 film _[Shrek](/wiki/Shrek "Shrek");_ DreamWorks CEO [Jeffrey Katzenberg](/wiki/Jeffrey_Katzenberg "Jeffrey Katzenberg"), who also served as producer, campaigned heavily for it to be awarded Best Picture, but it was not nominated in the category despite receiving nominations for a [Golden Globe for Best Musical or Comedy](/wiki/Golden_Globe_Award_for_Best_Motion_Picture_%E2%80%93_Musical_or_Comedy "Golden Globe Award for Best Motion Picture – Musical or Comedy"), [PGA Award for Best Theatrical Motion Picture](/wiki/Producers_Guild_of_America_Award_for_Best_Theatrical_Motion_Picture "Producers Guild of America Award for Best Theatrical Motion Picture"), [BAFTA Award for Best Film](/wiki/BAFTA_Award_for_Best_Film "BAFTA Award for Best Film"), and [Critics' Choice Awards for Best Picture](/wiki/Critics%27_Choice_Movie_Award_for_Best_Picture "Critics' Choice Movie Award for Best Picture") (and was the first animated film nominated in the latter three categories).[61][62][63] Similarly, the 2008 film _[WALL-E](/wiki/WALL-E "WALL-E")_ received many accolades and garnered speculation that it might be nominated for Best Picture, but it was instead nominated for six categories, tying with _Beauty and the Beast_ as the most nominated animated films in Oscar history, and won the award for Best Animated Feature Film.[64][65][66][67] Other animated films that garnered Best Picture speculation but were ultimately not nominated include _[Guillermo del Toro's Pinocchio](/wiki/Guillermo_del_Toro%27s_Pinocchio "Guillermo del Toro's Pinocchio")_ ,[68] _[The Boy and the Heron](/wiki/The_Boy_and_the_Heron "The Boy and the Heron")_ ,[69] and _[Spider-Man: Across the Spider-Verse](/wiki/Spider-Man:_Across_the_Spider-Verse "Spider-Man: Across the Spider-Verse")_[70] with the former two films winning Best Animated Feature Film at back-to-back ceremonies. 

### _Extremely Loud & Incredibly Close_ nomination

[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit&section=20 "Edit section: Extremely Loud & Incredibly Close nomination")]

Further information: [Extremely Loud & Incredibly Close (film) § Best Picture nomination controversy](/wiki/Extremely_Loud_%26_Incredibly_Close_\(film\)#Best_Picture_nomination_controversy "Extremely Loud & Incredibly Close \(film\)")

Critics and audiences criticized _[Extremely Loud& Incredibly Close](/wiki/Extremely_Loud_%26_Incredibly_Close_\(film\) "Extremely Loud & Incredibly Close \(film\)")_'s nomination for Best Picture, with some calling the film one of the worst Best Picture nominees ever.[71] Chris Krapek of _[The Huffington Post](/wiki/The_Huffington_Post "The Huffington Post")_ wrote very negatively about the film's nomination, calling the film "not only the worst reviewed Best Picture nominee of the last 10 years, [but] easily the worst film of 2011".[72] _[Paste](/wiki/Paste_\(magazine\) "Paste \(magazine\)")_ Magazine's Adam Vitcavage called the film's consensus for a Best Picture nominee "certainly the worst for at least 28 years",[73] and David Gritten of [_The Telegraph_](/wiki/The_Telegraph_\(UK\) "The Telegraph \(UK\)") called the nomination "mysterious".[74]

### _Emilia Pérez_ nomination

[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit&section=21 "Edit section: Emilia Pérez nomination")]

The nomination of _[Emilia Pérez](/wiki/Emilia_P%C3%A9rez "Emilia Pérez")_ for Best Picture, among other categories, was heavily criticized. Critics took issue with the film's portrayal of trans characters,[75] and its portrayal of [Mexican culture](/wiki/Culture_of_Mexico "Culture of Mexico"), including director [Jacques Audiard](/wiki/Jacques_Audiard "Jacques Audiard")'s claims about the [Spanish language](/wiki/Spanish_language "Spanish language") being "a language of developing countries, it's a language of countries of few means, of poor people, of migrants."[76]

[Karla Sofía Gascón](/wiki/Karla_Sof%C3%ADa_Gasc%C3%B3n "Karla Sofía Gascón"), who plays the titular role, accused the team of fellow Best Picture nominee _[I'm Still Here](/wiki/I%27m_Still_Here_\(2024_film\) "I'm Still Here \(2024 film\)")_ (as well as its lead, fellow [Best Actress](/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Actress "Academy Award for Best Actress") nominee [Fernanda Torres](/wiki/Fernanda_Torres "Fernanda Torres")) of running a [smear campaign](/wiki/Smear_campaign "Smear campaign") against her and _Emilia Pérez_ ,[77] which is explicitly against AMPAS's rules of campaigning.[78] The accusations were found to be baseless and itself perceived as an attempt to smear Torres's and _I'm Still Here_ 's reputation. As attention grew around Gascón, a series of [tweets](/wiki/Tweet_\(social_media\) "Tweet \(social media\)") in which she made several bigoted comments, of racist and Islamophobic nature,[79] were unearthed by Canadian journalist Sarah Hagi.[80] The majority of Oscar pundits agreed that this marked the end of _Emilia Pérez_ 's, the year's most nominated film, chances of winning Best Picture.[81]

## Winners and nominees

[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit&section=22 "Edit section: Winners and nominees")]

In the list below, winners are listed first in the gold row, followed by the other nominees.[6] Except for the early years (when the Academy used a non-calendar year), the year shown is the one in which the film first premiered in [Los Angeles County, California](/wiki/Los_Angeles_County,_California "Los Angeles County, California"); normally this is also the year of first release, but it may be the year after first release (as with _[Casablanca](/wiki/Casablanca_\(film\) "Casablanca \(film\)")_ and, if the film-festival premiere is considered, _[Crash](/wiki/Crash_\(2004_film\) "Crash \(2004 film\)")_ and _[The Hurt Locker](/wiki/The_Hurt_Locker "The Hurt Locker")_). This is also the year before the ceremony at which the award is given; for example, a film exhibited theatrically during 2005 was eligible for consideration for the 2005 Best Picture [Oscar](/wiki/Academy_Awards "Academy Awards"), awarded in 2006. The number of the ceremony (1st, 2nd, etc.) appears in [parentheses](/wiki/Bracket "Bracket") after the awards year, linked to the article on that ceremony. Each individual entry shows the title followed by nominee. 

Until 1950, the Best Picture award was given to the production company; from 1951 on, it has gone to the producer or producers. The Academy used the producer credits of the [Producers Guild of America](/wiki/Producers_Guild_of_America "Producers Guild of America") (PGA) until 1998, when all five producers of _[Shakespeare in Love](/wiki/Shakespeare_in_Love "Shakespeare in Love")_ made speeches after its win.[9][10] A three-producer limit has been applied some years since.[10][11] There was controversy over the exclusion of some PGA-credited producers of _[Crash](/wiki/Crash_\(2004_film\) "Crash \(2004 film\)")_ and _[Little Miss Sunshine](/wiki/Little_Miss_Sunshine "Little Miss Sunshine")_.[11] The Academy can make exceptions to the limit, as when [Anthony Minghella](/wiki/Anthony_Minghella "Anthony Minghella") and [Sydney Pollack](/wiki/Sydney_Pollack "Sydney Pollack") were posthumously among the four nominated for _[The Reader](/wiki/The_Reader_\(2008_film\) "The Reader \(2008 film\)")_.[13] However, now any number of producers on a film can be nominated for Best Picture, should they be deemed eligible. 

For the first ceremony, three films were nominated for the award. For the following three years, five films were nominated for the award. This was expanded to eight in 1933, to ten in 1934, and to twelve in 1935, before being dropped back to ten in 1937. In 1945, it was further reduced to five. This number remained until 2009, when the limit was raised to ten; it was adjusted from 2011 to 2020 to vary between five and ten, but has been a full ten since 2022. 

For the first six ceremonies, the eligibility period spanned two calendar years. For example, the 2nd Academy Awards presented on April 3, 1930, recognized films that were released between August 1, 1928, and July 31, 1929. Starting with the 7th Academy Awards, held in 1935, the period of eligibility became the full previous calendar year from January 1 to December 31. This has been the rule every year since except 2020, when the end date was extended to February 28, 2021, due to the COVID-19 pandemic, and 2021, which was correspondingly limited to March 1 to December 31. 

Since 2023, the category's winners and nominees from the 1927/28 and 1928/29 ceremonies have entered the [public domain](/wiki/Public_domain "Public domain").[82]

## Production companies and distributors with multiple nominations and wins

Columbia Pictures and United Artists have the most wins with 12, while 20th Century Fox has the most nominations with 64.

## Notes

[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Academy_Award_for_Best_Picture&action=edit&section=39 "Edit section: Notes")]

  1. **^** The 2nd Academy Awards is unique in being the only occasion where there were no official nominees. Subsequent research by AMPAS has resulted in a list of _de facto_ nominees, based on records of which films were evaluated by the judges at the time.
  2. **^** The film as produced by [Carl Laemmle Jr.](/wiki/Carl_Laemmle_Jr. "Carl Laemmle Jr."), but the award was given to his father [Carl Laemmle](/wiki/Carl_Laemmle "Carl Laemmle").
  3. **^** The Academy also announced that _A Farewell to Arms_ came in second, and _Little Women_ third.
  4. **^** The Academy also announced that _The Barretts of Wimpole Street_ came in second, and _The House of Rothschild_ third.
  5. **^** The Academy also announced that _The Informer_ came in second, and _Captain Blood_ third.
  6. **^** The eligibility period for the 93rd ceremony was extended through to February 28, 2021, due to the impact of the [COVID-19 pandemic](/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic "COVID-19 pandemic").
  7. **^** As a consequence of the eligibility period for the 93rd ceremony being extended through to February 28, 2021, the eligibility period for the 94th ceremony was truncated to ten months, running from March 1 to December 31, 2021.
